FOR YOU TO READ 

You've come far since you learned to spell. You've made habits that you'll be able to depend on for the rest of your life. The only piece of advice you need now is to review how to learn to spell a word. 

Everyone makes a spelling mistake now and then, but a good student tries not to make the same one twice. When you need to correct a mistake you have made, or keep yourself from making one, use the set of instructions below. 

STEP ONE 

See how the word really looks.

Look well at it to see it as a whole and to see what letters are in it. 

Then, if you don't know what it means, find it in your dictionary before you go any further. 

---

grad ù· a shun 

STEP TWO 

Say the word and hear how it sounds. 

Pronounce it carefully without looking at it. If it has more than one syllable, listen for them. 

Then check yourself by looking at the word again. 

If the pronunciation is difficult for you, get further help on pages 58 to 61 and 113 to 116. graduation

STEP THREE 

Look away and recall how the word looks.

Try to recall clearly how it looks and what letters are in it. Saying the letters softly may help. 

Then check with the word itself. 

---

STEP FOUR 

Copy the word correctly on paper.

Use as much care as you need to write it correctly. 

June 12 14.. 

Dear lincle Bob, Please come to my

Vraduatio Agrade STEP FIVE 

Write the word without looking at it.

Try to remember the letters and their correct order. 

Then check your spelling with the word itself. 

Use these five steps whenever you need special help in spelling.

TO HELP YOU USE THIS BOOK 

This book will give you several kinds of help in using words effectively. It will help you to become an independent speller and therefore a more effective writer. 

KEEPING MY SPELLING NOTEBOOK 

You will want to keep a Spelling Notebook. You may need to study some words more than once, to spell them right. Your teacher will help you make your Spelling

Notebook for Words I Must Study Again. In it you will spell right any Study List words you spelled wrong in your B and E tests each week, and any other words you spelled wrong in your other writing. Then you will be able to study these words and learn to spell them right. 

Your teacher will show you how to keep a part of your

Spelling Notebook for Words That Are Mine Now. This is the place where you will write all the words you misspelled in your review tests or in your other writing, when you have learned to spell them right.

FIVE THINGS TO DO EACH WEEK 

Here are the five parts of what you do each week.

A. Writing Words 1. Pronounce the Study List words. Your teacher, your dictionary, and pages 58 to 61 and 113 to 116 will help you. 

2. Read the story to find out how the words may be used. If there is a picture, find out what it tells about the story. 

3. In the story, find each Study List word or any word closely related to it. 

4. Write the Study List words on your paper. Pages iii to v will give you help if you need it. Check your work with the Study List. vi

B. Finding Words I Know 

1. Write the Study List words as your teacher pronounces them. Use this opportunity to improve your handwriting. 

2. Check your work with the Study List. Put a check √ after each word in your own list that you spelled right. 

Draw a line through each word you spelled wrong. 

3. If you spelled a word wrong, spell it right in your

Spelling Notebook. Write it under the heading B Test in Words I Must Study Again. Then check again with the Study List. 

C. Looking Into Words 1. Find things to remember about your Study List words by analyzing their spellings. 

2. Read the first thing you are to do when you are 

Looking into Words. Do it carefully. 

3. Analyze your other Study List words as directed. 

4. Use the rest of your time to study the words you missed in Finding Words I Know. Use the help you will find on pages iii to v. 

D. Using Words 

1. Learn more about what your Study List words mean by using them. 

2. Read the first thing you are to do in Using Words. 

Do it carefully. 

3. Then use your other Study List words as directed. 

4. Use the rest of your time to study the words written in your Spelling Notebook under Words I Must Study 

Again.

E. Making Sure I Know 1. Write the Study List words and the Review words as your teacher pronounces them. 

2. Check your words with the right Study Lists. Put a check after each word that you spelled right. Draw a line through each word you spelled wrong. vii

3. Spell your wrong words right in your Spelling Notebook. Write them under the headings New Words and

Review Words in Words I Must Study Again for next week. Then check again with the Study Lists. 

4. You may spell some words wrong in your other writing. Be sure to spell such words right in your Spelling

Notebook under Words from Other Writing in Words I 

Must Study Again. Make sure that you know them.

DEVELOPING INDEPENDENCE 

A good student becomes an independent worker as soon as possible. He loses no time in finding ways and places that will give him help. You can find several such ways and places in this book.

One way to become independent is to make everyday use of Words from Other Writing in correcting your spelling mistakes. Use Words That Are Mine Now to prove to yourself that you are becoming an independent speller. 

Places in this book where you can find extra help are: the five steps for correcting mistakes, pages iii to v; 

Making Our Work Easier, pages 119 and 120; and Dictionary Data, throughout the book. 

WHAT THE STARS MEAN 

Some of the things to do when you are Looking into 

Words and when you are Using Words are marked with stars (*). All students should do those things. Many students should do the other things, too. Your teacher will help you decide which ones you need to do. 

WATCHING HOW I GROW 

You will want to watch yourself growing in spelling. 

Your teacher will show you how to make My Progress 

Record. Mark on it the number of words you spell right in the E test of the new words of each week. 

The teacher will find directions for the use of the Glossary and both general and detailed pedagogic aid in the Teacher's 

Manual for Directed Spelling Activities and Guide to Spelling 

Progress. 

---

GUIDE TO SPELLING PROGRESS GRADE EIGHT 

Perhaps you don't realize how much you know about spelling. If you need proof, just start counting the words you can spell right. Before your list reaches more than one or two thousand words, you'll probably give up in surprise at your extensive knowledge of spelling. 

Now is the time to look ahead. What ways are you going to have next year, and all the years after that, of making sure you spell words right and not wrong? 

One wise thing you might do is use this year to analyze your spelling habits. Find out what efficient spelling habits you already have, and develop them still further. If you find you have certain bad habits, such as forgetting to notice all the vowels in a new word, or failing to use dictionary aids, set yourself to work changing the bad habits into good ones.

1. PERSONALITY AND SUCCESS A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words with your teacher. You will find all the Study List words, with their pronunciation, given in alphabetical order on pages 58 to 61. Then find the Study List words in the story. 

Sometimes the story will use a form of the Study List word, the root of it, or a word made from it. For instance, the word person in the story is the root of the word personality in the Study List. considerable considerably acquire interesting understanding misunderstanding advantage equipped advantages qualities publicity capacity publication essential personality analysis possibility influence possess responsibility 

What kind of person are you? What are your interests and capacities? What possibilities for success do you possess? Do you know how to take advantage of your good qualities and keep your bad ones from acquiring too much influence over your personality?

Our public schools consider personality training one of their important responsibilities to students. 

They realize that human beings are equipped very unequally with the essentials for successful living. 

A personality analysis often helps in understanding how to cure defects and improve on good points. 

2. Read pages iii to v carefully. Write the new words in alphabetical order on your paper. Check with the Study

2. THE PERSONALITY CLINIC A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words with your teacher. Then find them in the story. sympathy appeared individual attitude appearing confusion recognize apparently severe authority apparent inferior miserable disappear superior seriously demonstration unfortunate advisable inability Sometimes students have severe personality problems. These may appear in attitudes of inferiority or superiority. They may be demonstrated in a refusal to recognize authority. The student may be in a state of confusion about unfortunate home conditions. Many students are miserable about individual problems such as popularity or attractiveness.

The students' inability to solve their problems themselves may bring them to the personality clinic. 

Here they find sympathy and advice that help them to meet and overcome their serious difficulties.

1. Write two words that end with the syllables i ty, one that ends with ent ly, one that ends with pa thy, and one that ends with the three syllables ri ous ly.

3. SOLVING PERSONAL PROBLEMS A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words with your teacher. Then find them in the story. hesitate gradually reaction according harmony emergency accordingly situation regardless accordance conditions disposition management surround dispose courtesy attacked disposed courteous reverse We human beings deal with uncomfortable situations in several ways, in accordance with our individual dispositions. Some pleasant people manage to meet emergencies with self-control and quietness, and conditions soon return to harmony and comfort.

Other people's behavior is the reverse of this. They react to trouble by losing their tempers and attacking everyone within reach, regardless of courtesy or kindness. Such persons gradually become surrounded by a kind of danger zone, which their friends hesitate to enter for fear of stirring up discomfort. Which way do you solve your problems?

4. MAKING ADJUSTMENTS A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words with your teacher. Then find them in the story. accommodate accommodation probability necessarily unnecessary particularly accustomed adjustment adjust adjusted preference preferred enjoyable inclined traffic devoted sacrifice partial welfare activity 

Do you know anyone who does just as he chooses in every particular? In all probability you can't think of a single one. Most of us often have to accommodate ourselves to the activities and preferences of others, whether or not we enjoy it. Such adjustments are necessary for a satisfactory life. If you are inclined to dislike the idea, just think of those who constantly sacrifice their own comfort to yours.

Your family and friends always work for your welfare. Many others also are accustomed to serve you daily. Firemen, postmen, teachers, traffic officers and policemen, milkmen-this is only a partial list of those who devote themselves to you. Can you name others?

5. GREAT CONTRIBUTIONS A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. opportunities earnest changeable wisdom development scientific 

educate continually educational continuous additional investigation valued justify justified cease yield relieve universal The history of the world is a continued story of great personal sacrifices. Religion, medicine, science, education, art, all have their great names. More will be added in our lifetime. The schools of today will yield the famous and wise men and women of tomorrow, and opportunities for service will never cease to develop. Somewhere in the world there is always injustice, want, hunger, or disease. You find earnest workers there, too. They investigate causes, relieve suffering, change bad conditions into better ones. The value of such work is not limited to one country or race. Its benefit is universal. These men and women serve mankind.

6. A MODERN PUZZLE A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. marvelous progressive clothe requirements adequate enable required permanent ancient maintain existence vary maintenance exist various protection appetite variety entirely diet The essential requirements of human beings today vary very little from their ancient needs. We still need adequate food, clothing, and protection to enable us to maintain our existence.

The way man supplies his needs, however, has undergone a marvelous change. Most of the foods that tempt his appetite are entirely unlike those that once built his body and bones. The way he thinks about diet is changed, too. He clothes his body today as much for art as for comfort. And as for safety, he has made such progress in medicine, science, and education that he might almost expect to live in permanent security. That he does not is still one of our unsolved puzzles.

7. THE GIFTS OF CITIZENSHIP A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. congratulations satisfaction satisfactory satisfactorily administration acceptance spiritual accomplish persuade desired desirous democracy democratic adopt proposition adopted increased triumph increasing create We live in a democracy, and each citizen has a share in administering our government. This means that we ourselves help to create the kind of country we want. We accomplish this desirable result through freedom of speech and action.

When some public-spirited citizen has a plan that will increase the satisfaction, happiness, or usefulness of the population, it is his right to work for its adoption. He is free to use any legal means of persuading the people to vote for his proposition. He is also free to accept the rewards and congratulations that will come to him if his plan should triumph.

8. GOOD CITIZENS A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. civil politics civilization political organization familiar appreciate distinct appreciation elected appreciated scheme nevertheless strongly actually obligation realize obliged theory suitable

Our country needs and appreciates good citizens. 

No nation can be stronger than its people make it, and this is especially true in a nation organized like ours. We ourselves elect those who govern us. They therefore actually represent our opinions, and they have a distinct obligation to enact laws that are acceptable to us.

The individual citizen does not always realize how great a part he can play in the governmental scheme. 

He need not become a politician. Not everyone is suited for such service. He can nevertheless read and learn to understand the theories and principles of civil government. He can become familiar with current events. And then he will recognize the tasks he can do.

9. CITIZENS OF THE FAMILY A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. ✓ mutual occurred /income ✓available avail ✓domestic ✓ genuine allowance allowed ​dividends ✓behalf ✓ conference S confer arranged ✓corporation circumstances distribute ✓distribution wherein /council S Home can be a good place to learn citizenship. 

Many domestic duties need mutual effort. The home is like a small business corporation, wherein the family members are the officers. Each works on behalf of the others. Tasks may be distributed among the available workers according to the age and ability of each. In some homes periodic family conferences are arranged to discuss the income, budget, and possible allowance for each child. Such family councils are not useful under all circumstances; but where they can occur they pay dividends in genuine good citizenship.

10. HOW WE GET OUR MONEY A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. receipts automatic transaction banking ordinarily procure revenue undoubtedly merchandise refund financial merchant customary finance occupied mortgage security occupation honorable insured The world's financial problems are serious ones. 

The question of procuring funds occupies the attention of all who have bills to pay. We may obtain revenue honorably in a number of ways.

Money may be earned by labor. This is undoubtedly the way most of us receive it. Another customary way is to sell something people want. 

You may make the merchandise you sell, or you may buy it and resell it. A bank earns money by lending it at interest. This transaction ordinarily requires that the borrower own something of value, to insure repayment. He often gives a mortgage on his house as security for the loan. Then if he cannot repay, the mortgage secures the loan by providing for repayment. The repayment is not automatic, however, for the lender must go to court to collect. What other ways of getting money do you know?

11. OUR CRUEL BLESSING A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. urge urgent executed executive immense conceive delightful research laboratory warehouse tragedy desperate despair imagination agency tremendous data pursue criminal experiment Money has made tremendous differences in the world. It has been the agent of benefit countless times, and great crimes have been conceived and executed because of it. The loss of it has made men desperate, and a sudden gift of it has been able to delight them beyond any stretch of the imagination. 

It was the urge for wealth that led to the discovery of America and the settlement of immense sections of it. 

Money has made possible the pursuit of scientific experiments. It has bought data for research that has saved millions of lives, and it has paid for warehouses and laboratories in which wholesale destruction was prepared. It is the tragedy of money that evil and good must go hand in hand with it.

12. THE MONEY WE SPEND A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. proportion treasury certificate treasurer storage reduction obtained department hence convenient guarantee circulation convenience guaranteed circular inconvenience quantities substitute exceedingly utilize Money is an exceedingly convenient thing to have. 

We utilize it to obtain the things that satisfy and please us. It guarantees our supply of necessities; hence we value its usefulness.

In most countries the treasury departments issue paper money for public circulation. These paper certificates have no value in themselves. However, people use them with confidence because the certificates are guaranteed by the government, which has in storage a large supply of gold and silver. The paper money is a safe substitute for the gold and silver so long as the quantities of metal and paper money remain in proper proportion. A large increase of paper money or reduction of the metal supply might disturb the safety of our money.

Words have many little signs that help to make their meaning plain. Prefixes and suffixes are signs that have their own meanings. When we identify a familiar prefix or suffix in a word, we are halfway to understanding the whole word. The prefixes sub-, ex-, inter-, trans-, ante-, and dis- usually mean under, out, between, across, before, and down. Find them in the words below and note what useful keys to meaning they are. WARDROBES FOR WORDS A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. definition declare digestion confirm declaration employed confirmation applying employees affection applied affectionate application employment illustrate occasional applicant similar occasionally heartily Suffixes are even more useful to language than prefixes are. The words confirm, define, digest, and declare employ similar sets of suffixes. Some words even "wear" more than one suffix at a time. 15. FOLLOWING WORD CLUES A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. acknowledge acknowledgment acknowledged suspect readily instruction instructed acknowledging instruct remembrance conclude memorandum accurate construct construction thermometer meter structure postscript subscription 

A detective knows a suspicious sign when he sees one. He is a good guesser and makes accurate conclusions. Every reader can be a word detective and find familiar clues in new words. It's a big help with meanings. For example: 

We understand acknowledge when we see know in it. When we learn that struere is a Latin word meaning to build up, we are ready for structure, construct, and instruction. Knowing that meter has to do with measuring, and that thermo refers to heat, we are master of thermometer. Script, meaning writing, leads us to postscript and subscription.

The Latin word memor means mindful and makes memorandum and remembrance easy. The dictionary is full of detective stories like these.

DICTIONARY SERVICE A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. contemplate conscious concerning compared concern comparison concerned complex constitution convinced co-operation character co-operate correspond correspondent correspondence column criticism conscientious conscience

The dictionary has a complex character. Its writers conscientiously prepare information concerning the words of our language. Children often criticize the book's bulk, forgetting that this book cannot fairly be compared with any other. It constitutes our final authority in arguments on spelling, pronunciation, etc. A reference to its well-filled columns soon convinces us that we were wrong or right. 

But when we contemplate using the dictionary to help us decide on meanings, we become conscious of confusion. The dictionary corresponds somewhat to a city telephone directory, which gives names and street numbers but leaves us to find our way there ourselves. 

The dictionary gives many meanings for a word but no co-operation in choosing the one we want. We must do that work ourselves.

* 17. DICTIONARY SELF-SERVICE 

You can't use language efficiently without learning how. 

But from now on, you need not wait for anyone to teach you.

You can teach yourself, with the help of your dictionary. 

1. Alphabetical Order and Guide Words 

At the top of most dictionary pages are the two guide words. 

These are the first and last words explained on the page. Since all dictionary entries are arranged in alphabetical order, the entry you want can be found promptly by using the guide words. 

2. Spelling and Pronunciation 

You use words in two ways: in writing and in speaking. Either form can be verified in the dictionary, which provides two forms of spelling for each entry. The first spelling is correct for writing, the second for pronunciation. Syllables and accent marks are shown in both spellings.

Diacritical marks are used with the second form, or respelling, to indicate which sounds the letters of the word should have in pronunciation. The key words across the foot of the page show all the diacritical marks used in the respellings, in a separate word for each marked letter. 

3. Derived Word Forms 

A single word may be given extra forms by the addition of prefixes and suffixes. Such derived forms are sometimes shown as separate entries, with spellings, respellings, and definitions. 

Word forms that are irregularly derived from dictionary entries may be checked in the dictionary for spelling and pronunciation. 

4. Meanings 

Language serves a person according to the way he uses it. A word may mean something different every time it is used. 

Several possible definitions are listed for each entry. Many entries are further explained by illustrative examples showing how the words may be used. Pictures illustrate the appearance of many articles or places. Synonyms and antonyms provide words having nearly the same and nearly opposite meanings. The part of speech for each entry shows its use. 

5. Derivations from Other Languages 

Much may be learned about the meaning of a word by finding out its history. A good dictionary gives information about the language or languages from which each entry came down to our own language, how such early words grew into other words, and what they meant. 49

MATHEMATICS LITERATURE SCIENCE 

51

, Unit 17, continued character char'ac ter (kårꞌăk·tēr; kårʼík-), n. [L., an instrument for marking, character, fr. Gr. charakter, fr. charassein to make sharp,engrave.] 1. 

A brand or stamp, as one showing ownership or origin. 2. A mark or symbol used in recording language, as a letter; hence, writing or printing. 3. A quality or feature of a person or thing, esp. a distinguishing one. 4. Position; status; as, in his character as a son. 5. Reputation; as, he bears an excellent character. 

6. Moral excellence and strength; as, he is a man of character. 7. Á person having notable traits or characteristics; sometimes, Colloq., an odd or peculiar person. 8. One of the persons in a drama or novel. 

9. A testimonial to one's behavior, habits, etc.-Syn. See DISPOSITION. 

col'umn (kŏl'um), n. [OF. & L.; 

OF. colomne, fr. L. columna.] 1. 

Arch. A supporting pillar; esp., one with shaft, base, and capital, the shaft usually round. See ORDER, Illust. 2. Anything resembling such a pillar in form, position, or use; as, a column of smoke; the spinal column. 3. A military or naval formation in which the units of command, as companies or ships, are arranged one behind another. 4. a One of two or more upright sections of printed matter separated by a line or by a blank space. b A department of a newspaper, etc., usually one column wide, conducted by a special writer (columnist).—Syn. 

See PILLAR. com·pare' (kom·pâr′), v. t. [F. comparer, fr. L. comparare; see COM- 

PEER.] 1. To represent as similar; to liken. 2. To examine the character or qualities of, in order to discover likenesses and differences. 

3. Grammar. To state the positive, comparative, and superlative forms of (an adjective or adverb). concern com·par'i·son (kom·părți·sŭn), n. 

1. A comparing or condition of being compared; an estimate of likenesses and differences between two or more things. 2. Grammar. The modification of an adjective or an adverb, as by adding -er and -est or by prefixing more and most, to denote degrees of quality, quantity, or relation (positive, little; comparative, less; superlative, least). 

Most adjectives of one syllable, those of two syllables ending in -er, -le, -ow, or -y, and the most familiar of those having two syllables with stress on the last, usually form the comparative and superlative by adding -er and -est (slow, slower, slowest; dirty, dirtier, dirtiest; remote, remoter, remotest). 

Other adjectives, esp. those belonging to literary diction, and most adverbs, usually form the comparative and superlative by using more and most before the positive (beautiful, more beautiful. most beautiful). complex' (kõm·plěks'; kõmʼplěks; 

2), adj. [L. complexus, past part. of complecti to entwine around, comprise, fr. com- plectere to twist.]

GOOD WORKMANSHIP WITH THE DICTIONARY 

A carpenter does not usually own and operate a lumber yard, to supply his customers with the wood he uses in working for them. But if he is a master workman, he knows extra facts about his trade, such as where wood supplies can be obtained, or what kinds of wood are best for certain uses. With these extra facts he is better prepared to give service.

Many students discover that they can be better workmen by using the extra helps in the dictionary. They learn to search through it for explanations of things they may not understand. Are you a good dictionary workman? Can you understand or find out about everything in the dictionary excerpt on page 56? It has been borrowed for you from Webster's Students Dictionary. 

Do You Know These Things?

The Preface at the beginning of a dictionary gives you information about the ideas and materials that went into the making of it. It tells you such things as how many vocabulary entries the dictionary contains, how the materials are arranged, what types of materials you can find in the dictionary, and the like. 

A Guide to Pronunciation at the front of a dictionary explains in detail how to use the diacritical symbols in pronouncing words. It helps you to understand and use the key words across the foot of the pages. 

Rules for spelling and grammar may also be found at the front of the dictionary. These rules help you to remember to double the final g on the entry clog before you add the suffix ed or ing, and other such spelling aids as this.

Some dictionaries provide Explanatory Notes to give you further help in using the dictionary materials. They 54

tell you what to call the various items, such as entries, parts of speech, definitions, respellings, and the rest. 

The Abbreviations used in the dictionary are explained in a list at the front. There you will find that ME. means "Middle English," esp. means "especially," and so on. 

At the back of the dictionary you may find explanations of the hundreds of Abbreviations you may meet in your reading, such as those for the months and the days. 

You may find there an explanation of Signs and Symbols, such as the names of diacritical marks and punctuation marks.

At the back of the dictionary you may find also an alphabetical list of Proper Names of both places and people. In some dictionaries you may find out there what your own name means.

Can You Find These Things in Your Dictionary? 

Look at page 154 of Webster's Students Dictionary and notice the following items.

The two guide words. They are clock and clothing, at the top of the page.

Any main entry on the page. The ninth one is clois'ter. 

The respelling for any entry. The respelling for cloth´ing is (klōthing) 

It follows the first spelling of the entry.

Two possible pronunciations for clos'et. They are (klōz'ět; -it) 

The first syllable is pronounced the same in both cases; therefore only the changed second syllable is respelled. 55

A difference between the division of syllables in the first and second syllables of cloi'son né'. The second syllable of the respelling does not end in n, because in saying the word you do not pronounce the two n's. In writing cloi'son né', however, you must write both n's. Therefore both are given in the first spelling. cloi'son-né' (kloi'zo-na'), adj. [F., partitioned.] Inlaid between partitions; said of an enamel decoration laid on a metal or porcelain groundwork by setting the enamel between raised wire strips fastened to the surface. clois'ter (klois'ter), n. [fr. OF., fr. L. claustrum, pl. claustra, bar, bolt, bounds, fr. claudere, clausum, to close.] 1. A monastery or convent. 2. The re ligious, or monastic, life. 

3. A covered, usually arched and vaulted passageway along the walls of a court or a building.

Syn. Cloister, monastery, nun nery, convent, abbey, priory.

Cloister stresses the idea of seclusion from the world; convent, of community of living.

A cloister or convent for monks is 

Cloister. 

called a monastery; for nuns, a nunnery. A monastery or nunnery governed by an abbot or an abbess is an abbey; by a prior or prioress, a priory. -v. t. To confine in a cloister; to seclude from the world. - clois'tral (-trăl), adj.

18. HOW BIG IS YOUR WORLD? 

A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words with your teacher. You will find all the Study List words, with their pronunciation, given in alphabetical order on pages 

113 to 116. Then find the words in the story. submarine located dependent independent faithfully thoroughly independence extension location enthusiasm apparatus air liner enthusiastic competent curiosity magnificent parachute compass extraordinary voyage

Although money is useful and pleasant to have, life can be enjoyed without much of it. It all depends on how well you can see and hear. With the right kind of eyes and ears, you can voyage all over the world. 

You will need neither ship nor submarine, air liner nor streamliner, parachute nor elevator. What sort of apparatus do you need, then? It's all located within the narrow compass of your own head. It is your mind.

This magnificent piece of machinery needs to be thoroughly trained, however. It gives competent and faithful service only if you constantly extend its powers. You must look and listen with curiosity and enthusiasm for new sights and sounds. Your world will be bigger each time you find an extraordinary new fact.

19. GIFTS TO MANKIND A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. reckon continent artificial glorious glory communication enormous estimate remarkable issued remarks cable exhibition brilliant exhibit bulb inspiration airways electrical electricity There are some persons who have made the world bigger for all of us. They have had brilliant inspirations that have turned into glorious gains for mankind. Thomas Edison made an electric light bulb that gave better artificial light to the whole world. 

Cyrus Field promoted rapid communication between continents when he linked them with the transatlantic cable. The Wright brothers opened the airways to us with their remarkable exhibition of flying at Kitty Hawk. The first printed page that issued from Caxton's crude press opened the enormous treasure that lies in books. We cannot reckon or estimate the legacies such persons have left, for their gifts have influenced our actions and our thoughts.

20. TRAINING FOR LIFE A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. representative original kindergarten originally intelligent ambitious intelligence senior definite latest definitely seconded indefinite counsel privilege ideal provided provision appropriate university Our schools represent an educational ideal that is second to none in the world. It has grown from our country's original belief that all should have an equal chance to earn happiness. 

From the kindergarten all the way through senior high school, we are training for life. Opportunities are provided to help us learn what our abilities are. 

Then we have the privilege of choosing appropriate courses to take. Our teachers often give us valuable counsel in this. 21. OUR LIFEWORK A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. descend prospect typewriter efficient missionary preliminary efficiently competition graduate heretofore profession graduation herewith professional bookkeeping hereafter physician stenographer attorney opera Our ancestors knew a different world from the one their descendants occupy. Whereas heretofore workers outside the home were largely men, today both men and women carry on professions efficiently.

When we are graduated from high school we shall face the prospect of earning our living. Numerous fields of work are open to us. Let's take a preliminary look at them.

We may work with our hands, our minds, or both.

22. CONQUEST OF THE AIR A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. volunteer schedule transport transportation aircraft air mail technical endeavor seaplane inspection porter aerial hostess advertised highway forwarded forwarding assurance assuring vacancy A new field of endeavor is aviation. Air transportation has gone forward rapidly. Those taking to the aerial highways today have reasonable assurance of landing safely. Air accidents have been so advertised in the news, however, that some people consider all aircraft unsafe. 

Travel by airplane gives employment to many.

Some are pilots, hostesses, and others who actually go aloft. Some are mechanics who inspect each detail of the machinery; technicians who chart routes, plan schedules, and keep in radio touch with the planes themselves; porters who load luggage and help passengers. There is no lack of volunteers for the vacancies in this infant industry.

Dear Jack, Home, Tuesday

Here's the invoice that came with the duplicate propeller I had to get for my model plane. You can get the address of the company from it if you want to send for the mechanical glider they advertise. 

They'll send it C.O.D., or you can enclose your remittance in the order. Dad says he thinks they overcharge for their goods, but the materials they use are strictly Grade A. 

Dad and I took a horseback trip last week end out to the old Stone estate. Dad had to see some of the rural voters about the new highway commission. 

They were very cordial, and we had a good time. Yours, Jim

24. STRATOSPHERE TRAVEL A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. postpone qualified absolutely capitol install absolute introduced lieutenant altitude vicinity pressure committee liable unit liability license stratosphere sufficiently destination sufficient 

Dear Jim, March 12, 19Thanks for the address. It's in the vicinity of the capitol building, isn't it? Didn't we pass it, going to 

Mr. Hill's committee meeting in the capital last month? 

My cousin has qualified for his pilot's license. He's a lieutenant in the Guard. He lent me a book on stratosphere planes. They fly at such a great altitude that the thin atmosphere is a liability, and oxygen units must be installed to equalize the air pressure. 

Stratoliners have been introduced now. They get to their destination faster, but many think they haven't had sufficient testing to be absolutely safe. I wouldn't postpone a trip, though!

Jack

25. ALL THE WORLD MAKES MUSIC A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story.

release rhythm atmosphere marriage grief auditorium exquisite grieve divine fraternity musical friendship musician associate association medium orchestra cemetery awkward solemn One of man's oldest means of enjoyment is making or listening to music. All the world over, people find exquisite pleasure, or release from pain, through the medium of melody. Someone has called the violin the most divine of instruments, though most of us seem to associate the harp with heavenly harmony. 

Music adds joy to marriages and lessens the grief that follows a friend to the cemetery. Many schools in our country have fine modern auditoriums, where the kindergarten rhythm band performs as capably as the high-school orchestra. In all lands music promotes fraternity. It clears the atmosphere at awkward or solemn moments. Let us have music!

26. LETTING PEOPLE KNOW A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. plastics filed 

librarian files leisure assortment 

philosophy restaurant 

evidence cafeteria pamphlet evident mathematics bulletin evidently bureau sentiment

Writing was one of the very early human activities, and man has been writing in many lands for many ages. The world's libraries are full of the recorded evidence of his thoughts. Books and pamphlets flow from the printing presses in an assortment of literature ranging from philosophy and mathematics to cheap editions of sentimental novels.

The library filing system will help us to find any sort of reading material for study or leisurely enjoyment. We may choose a timely discussion of plastics, a textbook on running a restaurant or a cafeteria, or a bulletin from the state health bureau. Whatever man has written, man is interested in reading.

27. SAFEGUARDING OUR HEALTH A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. principle guardian distinguish ignorant treatment ignorance checked horror violent checking fierce violence measles sanitary condemn pneumonia rheumatism credit minimum impatient

Not very many years ago, the public generally was at the mercy of several diseases. If a fierce epidemic got under way, there was no sure means of checking it. People lived in horror of violent diseases such as smallpox, and the lack of the right treatment condemned millions to die in agony. In those early days the doctor was not a good health guardian.

At that time the public was ignorant of the principles of sanitation, ventilation, and disease prevention. 

Thus measles was almost as deadly as pneumonia, and rheumatism crippled many for life. 

Such conditions no longer exist. Great credit is due the distinguished physicians and scientists whose patient work is reducing disease to a minimum. 

Now we live in greater safety.

28. THE SAFETY CAMPAIGN A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. incident semester session substantial thief premium substance effective inventory percentage defective procedure per cent opposition proceed strenuous resolution indifferent merit scholarship 

Some schools carry on a strenuous safety campaign each semester. This is effective in preventing a substantial percentage of illnesses and accidents. 

Few parents are indifferent or opposed to such an important procedure.

Pupils like to share the responsibility for their own safety. They look around them and make lists of dangerous incidents and unsafe habits. In school and at home they take inventory of equipment and make special notes of defective items that should be replaced. Then at student sessions these findings are discussed and safety resolutions are passed. A premium is placed on safe living, just as rewards of merit are given for high scholarship. In this way, pupils learn that carelessness is often the thief that robs us of safety.

30. CHOOSING WORD MEANINGS A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story.

perceive ascertain elsewhere assistant judgment whereas involved eliminate approved approval specify specific specified inasmuch registered register registration duly tube trusting Many words do double duty. They have several approved meanings each, duly registered in the dictionary. But for assistance in true understanding of language we must look elsewhere. The dictionary gives all accepted meanings for a word, but it does not specify which is the right one. We must trust our own judgment to ascertain that. For instance, what is a tube? The single word tube might well puzzle us, whereas the sentence "The radio needs a new tube" solves the problem. The other words in the sentence have eliminated all but one meaning for tube. So we perceive that word meanings involve word groups, not single words, inasmuch as it is the use of a word that determines what it means at any one time.

31. WORDS THAT NEED WORDS A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. assigned canvass heir assignment adjourned fowl 

formal elastic brake seize petition moral leased omitted static lease pension stationary hangar Our ears bring us much of our language. Some words, therefore, could trick us if it were not for the words with them. People don't always speak clearly, and some words sound alike, anyway. Many pairs of words are confusing if spoken rapidly, as: partition, petition; attic, static; formerly, formally; adjoined, adjourned; admitted, omitted; mention, pension; moral, morrow; want, warrant; signed, assigned; elastic, plastic.

Homonyms could baffle us completely if we had neither companion words nor spelling to help us. 

Homonyms, you know, sound exactly alike, as: break, brake; canvass, canvas; seize, sees; stationery, stationary; fowl, foul; hangar, hanger; air, heir; least, leased. Words often need other words to help make meanings clear.

32. WHAT WE MEAN BY WHAT WE SAY A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. recommendation amendment opponent recommend resistance federal recommended chemistry economy institution chemical gratitude institute dormitory grateful refrigerator unanimous abstract respectfully shortage We may hear words clearly and know their spelling, and still not know what they mean. Many words are abstract, and we may not know what they mean to those who use them. Such words as chemistry, federal, dormitory, amendment, and unanimous have fairly definite limits. But you and your dad may mean quite different things by economize, respectful, or gratitude. A recommendation may be advice to one person and approval to another. A money shortage may mean poverty or only inconvenience. When you say refrigerator you may mean an ice or an electric one. An institution may be a custom to you and a school to your sister. Your resistance to advice may seem like opposition to one and courage to another. 

The dictionary tells only a part of the story of meaning.

33. A SERVICE LEAGUE OF WORDS A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. discretion rendered league convict¹ gossip prior patent initiation initial approximately approximate moderate candidate decent anniversary inquiry inquiries signature witness temperature 

¹Pronounced kon·vikt' or könʼvikt, depending on the meaning.

An inquiry into our language soon makes patent the fact that most words seem to belong to a service league. Some, such as gossip and prior, are not very useful in making other words. But small changes render most words able to do extra work. Sometimes the meaning is approximately the same, sometimes not. A shift of the accent changes the verb con- 

VICT into the noun CONvict. Changing the sound of a changes the adjective moderate to a verb. We can find sign in signature, annual in anniversary, initial in initiation, discreet in discretion. 

But a candidate is not always candid, a witness need not be witty, and there is no temper in temperature. Descent and decent look alike except for s, but they do different work. Let the Word Service League help you!

34. LETTER-SOUND SPELLING A. WRITING WORDS 1. This is your Study List. Say these new words. Then find them in the story. cylinder charity countenance comply chauffeur campaign compliance chorus colonial cancel campus client canceled congress compound cancellation compromise consequently conceal consequence 

Spelling is a puzzle! Our twenty-six alphabet letters conceal a much larger number of sounds, and consequently we must know how a word sounds as well as how it looks. For example, the letter c has two sounds, both of which appear in cancel; and the combination ch has three sounds, as in chauffeur, chorus, and charity. The letter s has four sounds: one in campus and congress, one in compromise and his, one in sure, and one in pleasure. The letter y has three sounds, as in cylinder, comply, and yes.

Vowels often combine to make compound sounds called diphthongs, as in coil, campaign, countenance.

Not all vowel combinations are diphthongs, however. 

Samples of this are seen in colonial, client, and coupon. You can learn many other sound-spelling facts by listening.

* 34. FAMILIAR DICTIONARY DATA 

Can you use all the dictionary data below? 

1. Finding an Entry 

All dictionary entries are given in alphabetical order. The guide words at the top of a page indicate the alphabetical range of the entries on that page. 

2. Spelling and Pronouncing Entries 

Each entry is presented in two forms of spelling. The first spelling is the authority for writing the word. The second spelling is the respelling, which is the authority for correct pronunciation. 

Syllables and accent marks are shown in both spellings. With the respelling, diacritical marks are also used. These are the symbols which indicate what sounds the letters of a word have in correct pronunciation. Each symbol may be checked with its key word at the foot of the page. 

3. Making Derived Forms of Entries 

Derived forms of words are made by adding prefixes or suffixes or both. Such forms help words to do extra work. Many such derived forms are given as separate entries in the dictionary. 

Irregular derived forms that are not entered separately are spelled for writing and sometimes for pronunciation also. 

4. Understanding What Entries Mean 

The dictionary offers several kinds of help with meanings. 

Possible definitions are listed for each entry. Illustrative examples show how an entry may be used. Pictured illustrations help the student to understand the meaning of many unusual or especially interesting words. Synonyms and antonyms provide meanings that are nearly the same as, or nearly opposite to, that of the entry. The part of speech indicates its work in a sentence. 

5. Finding the History of an Entry 

Our language represents a combination of many languages. In the dictionary you may find information about the history of each entry, including the language from which the entry came, how it grew, and what its early meanings were. 

Abbreviations are used in indicating the early history of entries. 

These and other abbreviations used in the dictionary are explained in a section at the front of the dictionary. 107

HOMONYMS 

A homonym is a word that sounds exactly like another word but has a different meaning and usually a different spelling. Below and on page 118 you will find a list of common homonyms, most of which you already know. Use this list for reference and class discussion. due dew air allowed e'er heir aloud altar alter earn urn ate eight eye

MAKING OUR WORK EASIER 

While we have been learning to spell, we have been learning also a few rules that can make our work easier. It will be wise to remember them and to keep them in a special place where we can easily refer to them. Here they are. 

1. PLURALS 

a. Plurals of most nouns are made by adding s to the singular forms. boy, boys bill, bills night, nights b. To make pronunciation easier, the plurals of some nouns are made by adding es instead of s. dress, dresses dish, dishes c. A noun ending in y after a consonant forms its plural by changing the y to i and adding es. baby, babies city, cities lady, ladies d. A few nouns make their plurals by changing their form, usually by changing the vowel. foot, feet mouse, mice 

2. CONTRACTIONS 

man, men calf, calves

An apostrophe is used to show the omission of a letter or letters in a contraction. can not, can't you will, you'll you are, you're 

3. ABBREVIATIONS 

An abbreviation is always followed by a period. 

Mister, Mr. 

Company, Co. pound, lb. 

4. POSSESSIVES 

Friday, Fri. yard, yd. gallon, gal. a. The possessive of a singular noun is formed by adding the apostrophe and s. 

John, John's mother, mother's dentist, dentist's b. The possessive of a plural noun ending in s is formed by adding only the apostrophe. boys, boys' girls, girls' 119

MAKING OUR WORK EASIER 5. SILENT E 

a. A word ending in silent e usually drops the e before a suffix beginning with a vowel. freeze, freezes cure, curable lose, losing chose, chosen b. A word ending in silent e usually keeps the e before a suffix beginning with a consonant. sure, surely advance, advancement nine, ninety care, careful, careless 

6. WORDS ENDING IN Y 

a. A word ending in y after a vowel usually keeps the y before a suffix. day, days obey, obeyed buy, buying b. A word ending in y after a consonant usually changes the y to i before a suffix, except a suffix beginning with i. try, tried (trying) fly, flier (flying) marry, marries (marrying) 

7. DOUBLING THE FINAL CONSONANT 

a. A one-syllable word ending in one consonant (except x) after one vowel usually doubles the consonant before a suffix beginning with a vowel. big, bigger, biggest run, runner, running b. A word of more than one syllable ending in one consonant after one vowel usually doubles the consonant before a suffix beginning with a vowel, if the accent is on the last syllable. be gin', beginner, beginning ad-mit', admitted, admittance 

LOOKING AHEAD 

120

GUIDE TO SPELLING PROGRESS A GLOSSARY FOR 

"GRADE EIGHT" This glossary was made to help you learn to use your dictionary. It has some of the things you will find in a real dictionary.

The spelling words of all the Study Lists in this book are given in dictionary order. The entry for each of these has at the end a number in brackets like this [23], to tell you the unit in which it is to be studied. Some other words are also given, to help you with your spelling words. There are no numbers in brackets after entries for these other words. 

For all entries, the spellings and respellings were taken from 

Webster's Students Dictionary for Upper School Levels, copy right 1938, by G. & C. Merriam Company, Springfield,

Massachusetts. For most words, illustration of their use is shown in sentence context. In the case of homonyms, homographs, words often confused, and words with more than one accepted spelling, the complete dictionary entries are taken, by permission, from Students Dictionary. You will want to own a real dictionary yourself as soon as you can. 

TO HELP YOU WITH PRONUNCIATION 

The first spelling in heavy black type shows you how to write the word. The respelling or respellings in parentheses will help you to say the word correctly.

In the respellings, marks have been used with some of the letters to show what sounds these letters have. The key words across the foot of each left-hand page will give you help in using these marked letters to pronounce words. 

Here is a list of marked letters used with your spelling words and the sounds you should give them: 121

ā as in save à as in always or in the first syllable of vacation 

â as in câre ŏ as in dog as in forget ǎ as in băg ǎ as in another ä as in farm å as in båsket ȧ as in ȧgo ē as in bē ē as in here é as in become ĕ as in fěd ĕ as in often ĕ as in after i as in find

TO HELP YOU UNDERSTAND MEANINGS 

Most words have more than one meaning. To help you with meanings in this glossary, most of the words are defined and some are used in sentences.

Words do different kinds of work in sentences. Abbreviations, such as adj., n., and v., are used in those entries taken from the dictionary to show how the word may be used. 

At the foot of each right-hand page after page 123, these abbreviations are explained. Your teacher will tell you about these abbreviations when you are ready to use them. 122

[25] at tach' (ă tăch'), v. t. [OF. atachier, fr. a to a word akin to E. tack a small nail.] 1. To seize and hold by legal authority, esp. under a writ, in order to force the payment of a debt; as, to attach a man's bank account. 

2. To bind; fasten; tie; connect. 

3. To assign; appoint; as, to attach an officer to the regiment as observer. 

4. To attribute; ascribe; with to; as, he attached no importance to the incident. - v. i. To be associated or joined; as, much interest attaches to his statement. at-tach'a ble, adj. 

Syn. Attach, affix, annex, append agree in the idea of joining one object to another. Attach is the most general term. Affix differs from attach in implying subordination of the second object to the first - an implication carried further in annex; as, to affix a stamp to an envelope; to annex conquered territory. 

Append implies that the object added completes, or assists in the full understanding of, the principal thing; as, to append notes to a chapter. Ant. Detach. 

[29] 

Attach looks almost like attack, but its spelling, meaning, and pronunciation are different. On the trip, we will attach a trailer to our car. The army will attack the town at midnight.

An attacking; onset; assault.

Syn. Attack, assail, assault all denote a forcible onset. Attack is the general term.

[31] break (brāk), n. A four-wheeled high carriage holding six or more persons besides the driver. break (brāk), v. t.; BROKE (brōk), Archaic BRAKE (brāk); BRO'KEN (bro'ken), Archaic BROKE; BREAK'ING. 

[AS. brecan.] 1. To separate sud denly or forcibly into two or more parts. 2. To open up the surface of, as land in plowing. 3. To disable, impair, or destroy as if by cracking apart; as: a To shatter. b To destroy the arrangement or firmness of; as, the troops broke ranks. c To defeat; as, to break a strike. d To crush the strength or spirit of; as, the news broke him. e To tame; as, to break a colt. f To bankrupt; ruin. g To degrade in rank or dignity; to dismiss. 

4. To lay or force open and to pass in or out; to penetrate. 5. To transgress or violate; as, to break the law; to break one's promise. 6. To disclose; as, he broke the news. 7. To interrupt; 

as: a To open (an electric_circuit). 

b To turn aside the force of; as, the branches broke his fall. c To discontinue; end; as, to break silence. 8. To exceed (a record). - v. i. 1. To crack bureau apart into two or more pieces, usually suddenly and forcibly. 2. To burst forth violently, as a storm. 3. To open by pressure from within, as a seed pod. into pieces; as: a To give way; yield 

4. To weaken or fail as if by cracking ground; as, the troops broke under fire. b To be crushed with grief. c To lose health or strength. d To become bankrupt. 5. To end friendly relations, as two countries. 6. To change one's course, gait, or the like, suddenly; as, the fox broke to the left. 

7. a To fail in musical quality. b To change from one register to another, as the voice. 8. To fall sharply in price. 9. To appear suddenly; as, a fire broke out; to appear on the skin; as, a rash breaks out. 

n. 1. A 

b An breaking; fracture. 2. A breaking or bursting forth; as, a break for freedom; the break of dawn. 3. A gap such as one made by breaking; hence; a A pause, as in conversation. omission in written or printed matter. c An abrupt change in direction or course. d U. S. A sharp decline in prices. e Slang, U. S. An awkward blunder; also, a chance, either good or bad; as, he had a lucky break.

du'pli-cate (dū'pli·kāt), adj. [L. duplicatus, past part. of duplicare to double, fr. duplex.] 1. Double; twofold. 2. Corresponding exactly to something else. 3. Designating a game, as at bridge, in which the cards are kept as dealt and played again by other players. - n. 1. Something that exactly resembles or corresponds to something else; a copy; counterpart. 2. Duplicate whist or bridge. (-kāt), v. t. 1. To double; fold. 2. 

To make a duplicate of. du'pli.ca/tion (-ka'shun), n. 

Syn. Duplicate, copy, transcript, facsimile, replica. Duplicate may be used of that which exactly corresponds to, or is the counterpart of, any object. A copy is a reproduction of something else, often without exact correspondence. Transcript is confined to the copy of a writing. A facsimile is an exact reproduction; it differs from duplicate in implying that the one object is an imitation of the other; as, two first folio Shakespeares may be duplicates, and two facsimiles of the first folio may be duplicates, but the facsimiles are not duplicates of the original. A replica is a copy of a work of art, properly one made by the maker of the original. [23] spell 

The letters duplicate three words. One of them does not sound like the other two, and all their meanings are different. Jerry had a duplicate copy of this book. Have the secretary make an extra duplicate of this letter. Can you duplicate this chair in your workshop?
